Wilshire Boulevard (16th Street New Traffic Signal) Safety impd. The 
second phase of implementation of the Wilshire Boulevard Safety 
Study will install transportation safety improvements at seven high 
crash intersection locations on Wilshire Blvd. The Wilshire Blvd 
Safety Study identified these locations based on community input, 
adjacency to schools and parks, and amount of crash incidents. The 
project will advance the Council Framework Priority Clean and Safe 
Santa Monica by reducing crashes, significantly enhancing crossing 
conditions for people walking, and improving connections to bus 
service.
03/01/26
Construction and 
implementation of Mobility 
active transportation 
projects utilizing grants,

Stewart Street & Pennsylvania Ave Safety Enhancement Project . This 
project will construct the Stewart/Pennsylvania safety enhancements 
to create a protected bikeway along Stewart Street, add new 
pedestrian scale lighting, add a new sidewalk on Pennsylvania 
Avenue, and a new median at the Nebraska/Yale intersection. The 
project will enhance pedestrian and bicycle safety along the corridor, 
create a complete street, and allow people of all ages and abilities 
access to the street.

Reactivate Miles Memorial

The Miles Playhouse was reactivated last winter as part of a soft

facility into the spring for a summer reopen. Cultural Affairs staff 
look forward to bringing a whole new array of new Arts & Culture 
programming to the Playhouse as part of a new RFP process to 
hire program partners (iC) to provide high end arts and culture 
programming for the FY 25/26 school year. Netwatch security 
monitoring system went live this past winter as well bringing real-
time monitoring, and greater safety and security to the facility. A 
new permanent fence is slated to be installed this summer 2025 with 
programming to follow.

Council Priority: Economic Opportunity and Growth

Target 
Completion 
Date
Citywide Banners Policy Evaluate and return with an ordinance and program to allow 
banners to be placed on City light poles supporting the arts and 
community cultural life of Santa Monica. The program should be 
designed as government speech, and allow for nonprofit submissions 
to promote arts and culture, pursuant to a program approved by 
the new Council. Program guidelines and details will be set forth 
in a resolution, with clear guidelines on the expressive action, such 
as government support and promotion of community-based artistic 
and cultural events and causes that serve civic and public interest. 
Council will adopt submissions by resolution, stating they reflect 
the views of the new Council. The City will maintain control and 
discretion over the selections and control installation. Staff will 
provide estimates of costs to the City for installation and program 
administration.

Cultural Grants – Art of 
Recovery model expanded to 
include microgrants
To increase reach of cultural funding, and ensure a quick turnaround 
from application to funding,  will launch a new Art of Recovery 
microgrants program expanding partnerships with Santa Monica 
communities, neighborhoods and arts producers who have not 
previously received an Art of Recovery grants. Microgrants are 
between $500 and $5,000 and Art projects must meet at least one 
of the following: Activate a park; Be a gathering, festival or event; or 
Activate a street.

Expand Community Aquatics 
programs and services
Expand and enhance recreational, educational, and enrichment

American Red Cross public safety courses. Manage room rentals and

6/30/26
Frieze LA Art Fair at the 
Airport
RAD CAD and Airport teams, in collaboration with other City 
Divisions, successfully worked with Frieze LA to bring their 
international art fair to the Santa Monica Airport on February 20-22, 
2025. Despite ongoing impacts from the Palisades Fire and concern 
from organizers about the safety of Santa Monica, the City was able 
to support a highly successful fair event and connect the art fair to 
local artists and arts organizations in need of support and leveraged 
opportunities to expand the arts at both the Santa Monica Airport 
and beyond. The Frieze partnership also resulted in the addition of a 
new work by an emerging Southern California artist to the municipal 
Art Bank collection. A fourth year is launching Feb 19, 2026.
2/19/26
Grants Programs – maintain 
arts grants programs, 
eventually grow
Cultural Affairs continues to manage the existing 6 grantmaking 
programs, distributing a total of $904,694 to artists and 
organizations. Develop grants growth plan to increase reach to all 40 
arts and cultural organizations licensed to work in the City of Santa 
Monica – we currently fund 27 organizations, and applicants do not 
receive their total requests. Grants are a core program for cultural 
affairs, ensuring nonprofits and individual artists are able to continue 
working and serving the Santa Monica community.

SANTA MONICA AT A GLANCE
94
Santa Monica: Populus Felix In Urbe Felici (Fortunate people in a fortunate place)
Santa Monica is a beautiful beachside community of 8.3 square miles, situated on the westside of Los Angeles 
County, about 16 miles from Downtown Los Angeles. 
i
t is bordered by the City of Los Angeles on three sides and 
the Pacific Ocean on the west.
HISTORIC TIMELINE
The city of Santa Monica sits on Tovaangar (Tongva for "the world"), which has been the home of the Tongva 
people since time immemorial. The area that we now call Santa Monica was first called “Kuruvungna” after 
the nearby natural springs; this remains as a sacred site to the Tongva peoples. Today, Santa Monica is home to 
a mix of commercial districts, residential communities, and recreation and arts venues. Each of Santa Monica’s 
character-rich neighborhoods – Pico, Wilshire Montana, Ocean Park, Downtown, Sunset Park, North of Montana, 
and Northeast – has a unique vibe. Recently named by National Geographic as one of the "Top 10 Beach Cities 
in the World" and by T
i
ME as one of the "Best Places to Live," Santa Monica features three miles of Pacific 
Ocean coastline, broad sandy beaches, and the iconic Santa Monica Pier. High-profile global technology and 
entertainment companies, small entrepreneurial start-ups, restaurants and retail businesses call the city home.
Santa Monica is a full-service city providing police, fire, emergency services, water, refuse and recycling, streets, 
parking, planning, building, engineering, free public Wi-Fi, a high-speed fiber optic network, parks, affordable 
housing, economic development, libraries, rent control, a regional transportation network, local access television 
channels, and recreational, cultural and educational services to the community.

SANTA MONICA AT A GLANCE
96
LOCAL ECONOMY
The City’s economy continues on a path to recovery in the aftermath of the COviD-19 global pandemic, changing 
consumer trends, increases in cost of living and inflation, legal costs, and the January 2025 Los Angeles wildfires.  
The city has made proactive investments to support local businesses, workers, and residents, including: adopting 
permanent outdoor dining and zoning changes to keep businesses open and workers employed; introducing 
Southern California’s first entertainment zone to the Third St Promenade to help support local restaurants and 
boost the vibrancy of the downtown;  welcoming 1,222 new housing units approved  for downtown; supporting 
free public art and cultural programming and events through the Art of Recovery program.
Even amid real challenges, Santa Monica maintains a very robust and diverse economy. Known as “Silicon Beach,” 
Santa Monica’s businesses are at the leading edge of the nation’s creative economy, venture capital and startup 
scene. People come from all over the world to work at companies like Activision, Red Bull, and  Snap.
From entertainment leaders like Universal Music Group and Lionsgate to startup successes like GoodRx, Truecar, 
and Beautycounter, Santa Monica is a hub for creativity and business innovation. Renowned healthcare providers 
like UCLA Medical Center, Providence St. John’s Health Center and Kaiser Permanente also call Santa Monica home 
and have played a pivotal role in the community’s care over the past three years. Santa Monica offers Class A 
office space with access to sun, surf, and a highly skilled workforce, along with a nationally recognized high-speed 
fiber optic network. Principal local employers are in the fields of education, municipal government, technology, 
fintech, health care, policy, and research. Santa Monica’s leading industry sectors are information Technology, 
Professional, Scientific and Technical Services, Accommodation and Food Services, and Health Care and Social 
Assistance (by employment).


===== PAGE 105 =====

FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BIENNIAL BUDGET
SANTA MONICA AT A GLANCE
97
Employers and employees are drawn to the area’s cultural, artistic, and recreational amenities. Unbeatable year- 
round sunshine and warm weather helps too! 
Tourism is an important driver of the local economy. Santa Monica attracts approximately 5 million visitors 
annually from around the world as well as regional day-trippers.  in 2023,, Santa Monica welcomed over 4.6 
million visitors who spent $938 million in Santa Monica, generating significant retail sales and transient occupancy 
tax revenues for the City.
5,747 jobs are supported by the tourism industry. The City’s 41 hotels typically operate with high occupancy rates 
year-round, apart from the recent dip due to COviD-19 and international travel restrictions.
Retail also plays a major role in Santa Monica’s economy. Santa Monica is home to a number of distinctive 
shopping districts. in the heart of Downtown, the Santa Monica Pier, Third Street Promenade, and Santa Monica 
Place draw locals and visitors alike. South of the Civic Center are the Main Street and Pico Boulevard districts, with 
eclectic shops, art, performance venues, and fine dining. On the north end of town, numerous boutiques and trendy 
restaurants line Montana Avenue, and Wilshire Boulevard provides an important thoroughfare throughout the city.
While our streets have had some quiet days over the last few years, residents, businesses, and workers are 
reemerging, and on an average day, tourists, shoppers, and employees significantly boost the daytime population 
to an estimated 250,000 people. The streets are now more vibrant than ever with the addition of outdoor dining 
activations throughout the city.
GOVERNMENT AND ADMINISTRATION
The City of Santa Monica was incorporated on November 
30, 1886 and subsequently adopted a City Charter in 1945. 
in 1947, a Council-Manager form of government was 
established following a vote of the City’s residents and 
approval by the California legislature.
The City Council consists of seven members elected by the 
community at large to four-year terms.
Elections are held every two years, at which time either 
three or four Council members are elected. 
The Council selects the Mayor and Mayor Pro Tempore 
each December. 
City Council appoints a City Manager to administer the 
City, a City Attorney and a City Clerk. The City Council 
also serves as the governing bodies for the Santa Monica 
Redevelopment Successor Agency, the Housing and Parking 
Authorities, and the Public Financing Authority.

FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BIENNIAL BUDGET
SANTA MONICA AT A GLANCE
98
SCHOOLS
The highly rated Santa Monica-Malibu Unified School District (SMMUSD) serves both Santa Monica and Malibu 
residents. There are 10 elementary schools, two middle schools, three high schools, and an alternative K-8 school in 
the district. The City of Santa Monica provides $24 million in funding support to SMMUSD annually.
Santa Monica College (SMC) is one of the state’s top two-year community colleges. SMC serves over 30,000 full-
time and part-time students on several campuses and offers more than 300 new courses in 58 subject areas. For 
28 years, the college has ranked #1 in transferring students to the University of California, University of Southern 
California, and other four-year campuses.

NATIONAL ECONOMY
U.S. Gross Domestic Product (GDP) was above average last year and is expected to slow in 2025 due to tariffs 
announced by the Trump administration but not fall into recession. Consumer spending, which comprises 
approximately 70% of GDP , was fairly robust last year. Consumers were buoyed by higher incomes, stock 
portfolios, and real estate prices. The Federal Reserve projects the consumer to pull back somewhat this year. 
inflation, the Consumer Price index (CPi), has declined materially from the peak in 2022. CPi remains sticky due in 
part to elevated home prices and insurance costs, and still above the Fed’s 2% long-run target. Economists expect 
inflation to increase in 2025 pursuant to tariff impacts.
The amount of funding recommended for programs and services is driven by available resources. Economic 
conditions at the national, state and local levels affect the level of revenue available to the City each year. This 
section identifies the existing economic conditions that influenced the development of the annual budget. The 
section includes summary charts for Citywide budgeted revenues and expenditures, including an overview of 
retirement expenditures and employee contributions. The section also presents summary information regarding 
the major tax revenues and their impact on the General Fund revenue forecasting, and revenue analysis for 
each fund.
Sources: Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics


===== PAGE 109 =====

100
The labor market remains robust, with an unemployment rate of 4.2%, which is below the lower limit of full 
employment (economists estimate the full employment range between 4.5% and 5.5%). The unemployment 
rate also remains very low from a long-term perspective. The Federal Reserve expects the unemployment rate to 
increase slightly by the end of 2025 to 4.4%.

The Fed Funds effective rate is currently at 4.33%, with market participants expecting the Fed to lower the Fed 
Funds rate to 3.5% by the end of 2025. However, recently Fed Chair Jerome Powell stated in light of the tariff 
uncertainty, “we are well positioned to wait for greater clarity before considering any adjustments to our policy 
stance. it is too soon to say what will be the appropriate path for monetary policy”.  
STATE ECONOMY
California's economy continues to be the largest in the United States and the fourth largest in the world, but 
its growth has slowed recently. While California's GDP has continued to increase, it has grown at a slower pace 
compared to the national average in recent years. Job growth has also slowed, and the state's unemployment 
rate at 5.3% has been higher than the national average. The state is adding fewer jobs per month as compared to 
previous years. While California has added jobs in key sectors like healthcare, retail, and transportation, it has also 
seen job losses in sectors like information technology and manufacturing. California’s housing prices increased by 
about 6% last year and are expected to continue to climb this year.

Sources: Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics
California's economic outlook for 2025 presents a mixed picture, with expectations of modest growth tempered by 
ongoing challenges. Some of those challenges may come from tariffs which may raise costs to many industries.
LOCAL ECONOMY
Santa Monica has traditionally benefited from a diverse tax base, relying less on sales and property taxes compared 
to many other cities. While these taxes remain significant, the local economy is heavily dependent on retail, tourism, 
and the hospitality sector—industries that faced substantial declines during the pandemic. Although visitor activity 
has been steadily recovering, the pace remains slow. Remote work trends continue to impact daytime population 
levels and foot traffic in Santa Monica, leading to reduced consumer spending in restaurants and retail stores, 
which in turn affects sales tax revenues.  Currently, Santa Monica's daytime population stands at about 60% of its 
2019 levels, with retail and corporate vacancy rates exceeding regional averages.
Transient Occupancy Tax (TOT) or Hotel Tax revenue recovery remains sluggish compared to pre-pandemic 
projections. Despite Measure CS, enacted in March 2023, which raised the TOT by 1% for hotels and 3% for 
homeshares, the rebound has been slower than anticipated. Recent projections indicate the city may not reach 2019 
peak tourism levels until late 2026 or early 2027, as tourism gradually rebounds. Sales Tax trends reflect reduced 
consumer spending in luxury and electric vehicle sales, driven by affordability challenges such as a 29% rise in car 
prices since the pandemic, higher financing costs, interest rates, and insurance premiums. Restaurants, especially 
fine dining establishments, have also been affected, with consumers opting for more budget-friendly options. 


===== PAGE 112 =====

103
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
BUDGET OvERviEW - OvERALL ECONOMiC CONDiTiONS
Parking revenues—a key visitor-related revenue source—show a moderate annual growth of 1.75%, reflecting 
pandemic-driven shifts like online shopping and remote work, which are expected to limit parking demand in the 
future. Additionally, increased adoption of alternative transportation methods such as rideshares, public transit, 
bicycles, scooters, and free downtown parking for the first 90 minutes may further impact parking revenue. A 
comprehensive citywide parking study, including supply and demand analysis, is underway, with findings expected 
by late 2025. 
Together, TOT, sales taxes, and parking-related revenues (including Parking Facility Taxes) make up 
46% of General Fund revenues.
Property Taxes have remained strong, supported by rising assessed values during the pandemic. While this 
growth is expected to continue, it may decelerate due to a softening real estate market influenced by higher 
interest rates. Business License Tax revenue from business license taxes has surpassed pre-pandemic levels. in 
November 2024, voters approved Tax Measure F , which modernizes the outdated business license tax structure. 
The measure exempts small businesses, reduces rates for most retailers and restaurants, raises the corporate 
headquarters rate to 0.25%, removes auto dealer tax exemptions, and reinstates a business license processing fee. 
These changes, being phased in over three years, are projected to generate an additional $3 million annually for 
the General Fund.
Sources: Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics


===== PAGE 113 =====

104
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
BUDGET OvERviEW - OvERALL ECONOMiC CONDiTiONS
Santa Monica’s 10% Parking Facilities Tax (PFT), established in 1993, applies to all public and private parking 
facilities. in 2024, voters approved Tax Measure K, increasing the PFT by 8% for privately owned facilities, bringing 
the total tax to 18%. This adjustment is expected to generate $6.7 million annually to support city services. City-
owned parking lots remain exempt, maintaining the original 10% tax rate.
Looking forward, total General Fund revenues are projected to grow by 3.2% in FY 2025-26 compared to the prior 
fiscal year's adjusted estimated actuals of $455.9 million, with an average annual increase of 3.7% over the next 
few years.
A more detailed discussion of local taxes follows the Citywide Budget overview.

TOTAL FY 2025-26 CITY REVENUES: $810.2 MILLION
Total projected City revenues for FY 2025-26 are 
$810.2 million, net of reimbursements and transfers. 
This represents a $34.8 million or 4.5% increase from 
the FY 2024-25 revised budget. The increase reflects 
greater General Fund revenues primarily from Parking 
Facility, Property taxes, and advertising revenue from 
the City’s Digital Wayfinding Kiosk program, reflecting 
an entire year of revenues from full implementation of 
the program, as well as moderate growth projected for 
Transient Occupancy tax revenue from some return of 
visitors and tourism due to anticipated major events in 
the region. in the other funds, revenues reflect increased 
grant and capital project expense reimbursements from 
funding agencies, approved solid waste rate adjustments, 
anticipated water and wastewater rate adjustments, 
and anticipated funding allocations from various other 
government agencies. Revenue details and projections for 
the next two fiscal years for all City funds are detailed in 
the Revenue Analysis section.

represents a $44.7 million or 5.5% increase from 
FY 2025-26, which includes greater General Fund 
revenues from continued implementation of the 
Business License Tax rate changes, moderate growth 
in Property Taxes, as well as slight growth in Sales 
Tax and Transient Occupancy tax from some return

TOTAL FY 2025-26 CITYWIDE EXPENDITURE BUDGET: $789.9 MILLION
The FY 2025-26 budget includes $789.9 million in 
expenditures, net of reimbursements and transfers, for all 
funds. This amount remains relatively level with the revised 
budget for FY 2024-25, primarily reflecting maintenance 
of effort and cost increases in the operating budget for 
compensation, health benefits, pension, insurance and 
operating expenses, such as utilities and fuels. Also included 
are operating subsidies and loans to the Pier Fund and 
Beach Fund, respectively, and adjustments for estimated 
grant funded operating and capital project expenditures. The 
increases are offset by reduced expenditure appropriations 
from the Measure GS Fund — FY 2025-26 expenditures will 
be programmed later in the fiscal year, once the revenue 
reporting period for FY 2024-25 has been reconciled and 
revised available balances are determined.
TOTAL FY 2026-27 CITYWIDE EXPENDITURE BUDGET: $825.5 MILLION
The FY 2026-27 budget includes $825.5 million in 
expenditures, net of reimbursements and transfers, for all 
funds. This amount represents a net increase of 4.5% from 
FY 2025-26, primarily due to an increase in programmed 
capital expenditures in the Stormwater, Water, Pier, Big Blue 
Bus, and General Funds. Operating expenditures continue 
to reflect maintenance of effort and cost increases in the 
operating budget for compensation, health benefits, pension, 
insurance and operating expenses, and operating subsidies 
and loans to the Pier Fund and Beach Fund, respectively.
The difference between Citywide revenues and expenditures 
is primarily due to disparities in timing between revenues 
and expenditures for capital and grant funded projects. 
The variance in the General Fund is addressed through the 
one-time use of working capital reserves. The General Fund’s 
revenue streams remain affected by the ongoing economic 
downturn triggered by the COviD-19 pandemic, with the retail, tourism, and hospitality sectors—key contributors 
funding city services—continuing to experience setbacks.
As in previous years, City revenues are subject to changes in economic conditions, State budget actions, and 
legislative changes.

OTHER FUNDS
in addition to the General Fund, the City budget includes a number of funds that are either self-sustaining or 
require assistance (subsidies or loans) from the General Fund, as projected upon completion of five-year financial 
forecasts for all funds.
The Water and Wastewater Funds will maintain positive balances throughout the five-year forecast period 
based on proposed rate adjustments directed by City Council on March 25, 2025, and scheduled to be adopted 
at the June 10, 2025, public hearing.  Both funds are making progress on a number of projects that will increase 
the locally sourced water supply and reduce the City’s reliance on imported water.  The projects support Reservoir 
improvements, Groundwater Well improvements, and the Arcadia Water Treatment Plant Operations Building 
Replacement in the Water Fund and improvements to the Moss Avenue Pump Station and Santa Monica Urban 
Runoff Recycling Facility (SMURFF) upgrades in the Wastewater Fund.  Historically, imported water has cost as 
much as 30% more than local water.  By continuing to invest in the local water supply, the City diversifies and 
enhances its drought resiliency and provides long-term cost savings and cost certainty for the community.  Staff 
recently completed a five-year Water and Wastewater rate study, conducted every five years, to align rates with 
the projected costs to provide water service. if approved by Council at the June 10, 2025 public hearing, the rate 
adjustments will take effect in July 2025. 
The Resource Recovery and Recycling (RRR) Fund will remain self-sufficient through FY 2029-30, partially 
attributed to the infusion of one-time funds from the closeout of old construction and demolition projects. The 
fund has experienced unexpected increases in expenses due to the closure of the Chiquita Canyon Landfill, partial 
relocation of RRR operations and rent payment to the Airport, increased costs for household hazardous waste 
collection services, and an increased reimbursement to the General Fund for utility billing services. As such, it will 
likely take the RRR Fund additional time beyond the current forecast period to build up to the 90-day reserve level 
as recommended by the 2023-28 Five-Year Solid Waste Rate Study. Staff will start planning for the next rate study 
in FY 2026-27.
The Department of Transportation’s (DOT) Big Blue Bus (BBB) Fund biennial budget reflects the department’s 
continued commitment to providing safe, efficient, and reliable transportation to the community. Over the FY 
2025-27 biennial period, the department will make strategic investments which aim to improve BBB service 
delivery and routes, as well as increasing system-wide safety for transit riders and front-line staff. DOT will proudly 
make notable advancements in the electrification of the BBB fleet by receiving forty-five battery electric buses, 
which will expand its zero-emissions buses to 33% of the total fleet. Additionally, DOT will begin constructing 
and completing multiple phases of the electrical infrastructure needed to support its fleet conversion. BBB will 
maintain a balanced budget over the forecast period. 
The Airport Fund is projected to continue to have a healthy fund balance throughout the forecast period. 
Although revenues will decrease in FY 2025-26 mostly due to newly negotiated tenant lease credits, staff expects 
revenue to increase in future years.  Staff is moving forward on several previously postponed capital improvement 
projects in FY 2025-26 and FY 2026-27, rehabilitation of Airport parking lots, and renovations to Airport buildings 
and facilities to enhance lease revenue opportunities.  Airport closure is planned for all aircraft operations after 
December 31, 2028.

110

The Community Broadband Fund was incorporated into the General Fund, effective July 1, 2023. 
The Beach Recreation Fund, which supports Beach and Beach House operations, is projected to remain in a 
structural deficit during the forecast period through FY 2029-30. The fund was impacted by pandemic closures and 
has required $7.9 million in advances from the General Fund and a $1.45 million loan from the Special Revenue 
Source Fund since June 30, 2021. in addition, the Beach Fund is expected to require advances totaling nearly $8 
million during the biennial period, in part due to expected increases in the contract with Los Angeles County for 
lifeguard services.  The primary source of revenue for the Beach Fund is parking revenues, which have returned 
to pre-COviD levels. However, parking revenues will be impacted beginning in FY 2025-26 for construction 
staging related to the Pier Bridge Replacement project; all the pier deck parking and half of Beach Lot 1N will be 
impacted for two years, with a potential loss of up to $4 million in parking revenue. Every effort will be made to 
minimize the extent of the impact. Staff will revisit rate increases upon completing the citywide parking rate study, 
which is currently underway. Parking rate increases depend on California Coastal Commission (CCC) approvals, 
a prerequisite for implementing rate increases. $3.5 million in identified capital projects remain unfunded. in 
addition to the advances noted above, the fund is projected to require additional General Fund advances of 
$20 million for a total of $28 million between FY 2024-25 through FY 2029-30 if a parking rate increase is not 
implemented. Staff continues to seek efficiencies and revenue-generating opportunities to support unfunded 
capital expenditures and address the structural deficit.
The Cemetery Fund will maintain a positive fund balance throughout the five-year forecast period. Although 
there is a decrease in the fund balance, it is due to additional capital improvement projects that are in place to 
maintain the mausoleum infrastructure and enhance the cemetery grounds. Additionally, the FY 2025-27 biennial 
budget assumes a fee increase to offset the increased cost of landscaping services and other miscellaneous costs.  
Staff continues to assess overall Cemetery operations, projected plot sales, and reconfiguration and development 
of the Cemetery area to extend inventory and maintain fiscal sustainability.
The Housing Authority Fund is stable and will maintain a positive fund balance throughout the forecast period. 
The additional administrative funding provided through the CARES Act for the Santa Monica Housing Authority 
in FY 2021-22 supplemented traditional Section 8 administrative funding. This allowed the fund to build an 
administrative cash reserve and to ensure a positive fiscal outlook through the five-year forecast window.
The Pier Fund typically generates enough revenue to cover operating costs, but the fund cannot support the 
significant capital investment required to regularly maintain and upgrade the infrastructure. There are $19 million 
in unfunded Pier capital projects and it is projected that the fund will require General Fund subsidies of $9 million 
over the five-year forecast period (through FY 2029-30).  The primary revenue sources for the Pier Fund are lease 
payments and parking income. Revenues have seen a decrease, possibly attributable to impacts from the Palisades 
Fire, and SMPD directives to close portions of the Pier Deck; however, revenues have been bolstered by parking lot 
event rentals.   The Pier Bridge Replacement (funded through the Caltrans Highway Bridge Program) will impact 
parking revenues once construction begins in FY 2025-26, as the Pier Deck parking lot will be closed.   Staff 
continues to seek efficiencies and revenue-generating opportunities to support unfunded capital expenditures and 
to address the structural deficit. 


===== PAGE 120 =====

111
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
BUDGET OvERviEW - CiTYWiDE BUDGET OvERviEW
The Measure GS Fund was established following the voter-approved real estate transfer tax measure adopted 
in November 2022 and implemented beginning March 1, 2023. This fund supports two primary areas: public 
education and homelessness prevention and affordable housing initiatives in the City of Santa Monica. Measure 
GS imposes a tax rate of $56 per $1,000 for properties sold or transferred at $8 million or more.
Annually, the first $10 million in revenue supports the Santa Monica-Malibu Unified School District (SMMUSD), 
while the next $40 million is allocated to affordable housing and homelessness prevention efforts. Revenue 
beyond $50 million is distributed with 80% allocated to housing and homelessness initiatives and 20% to public 
education. A community-based Oversight Committee guides the allocation of these funds to ensure alignment with 
community priorities and equity principles. The fund is expected to remain fiscally sustainable over the forecast 
period, with revenues closely tied to activity in the high-value real estate market.
The Stormwater Management Fund receives revenues primarily from user fees and the Special Revenue 
Source Fund receives revenue from various sources, which are restricted and committed for specific uses. Both of 
these funds will generate adequate revenues to remain sustainable throughout the forecast period.
The Vehicle Management Fund and Information Technology Replacement & Services Fund will maintain 
positive balances throughout the forecast period. However, to maintain a positive balance, the funds have had to 
defer scheduled replacement and maintenance of equipment, primarily due to a two-year suspension/reduction 
in FY 2020-21 and FY 2021-22 in funding from the General Fund associated with revenue shortfalls from the 
pandemic. in the vehicle Management Fund, the suspension totaled $8.8 million and resulted in the majority 
of the 227 vehicles scheduled for replacement to be deferred to FY 2022-23 and beyond. in the information 
Technology Replacement & Services Fund, the suspension totaled $3.4 million and resulted in the deferment of all 
computer equipment replacements for two years beyond their scheduled replacement.  This is not sustainable, and 
funding needs to be restored. As General Fund revenues continue to gradually improve, funds are being earmarked 
to immediately address areas that cannot withstand the replacement timing lag and to gradually resume the 
replacement schedules. The General Fund will begin partial repayment to the information Technology Replacement 
& Services Fund beginning in FY 2027-28.
CITYWIDE TOTAL COMPENSATION
When determining total compensation, the City includes employee salaries, pension contributions, health care and 
workers’ compensation costs.  These items make up approximately 54% of the City’s overall operating budget, 
and approximately 67% of the General Fund operating budget. Healthcare costs make up approximately 10% 
of the total budgeted compensation. Pension costs make up 21% of the total budgeted compensation.  Workers’ 
compensation costs, which are not a negotiated benefit but are largely driven by State mandates, make up 
5% of total budgeted compensation. Further discussion of pension costs follows. To accommodate healthcare, 
pension, and workers’ compensation costs, cost of living adjustments (COLA) for employees consider the total 
compensation package and not just salary.

PENSION CONTRIBUTIONS
The City’s defined benefit pension plan, Santa Monica Public Employees’ Retirement Plan, provides retirement and 
disability benefits, annual cost of living adjustments, and death benefits to plan members and beneficiaries. The 
plan is part of the Public Agency portion of the California Public Employees’ Retirement System (CalPERS), which 
acts as a common investment and administrative agent for participating public employers within the State of 
California. Active full time employees in the plan reimburse the City for a portion of the costs. The overall level of 
annual contributions by City employees, using negotiated contribution levels as of June 30, 2022, is approximately 
27.5% of the total annual cost of pensions, while the percent contributed varies by plan (Police sworn, Fire sworn, 
Miscellaneous employee) and the length of time an employee has been with the City (Miscellaneous employees 
hired before July 2012 and public safety employees hired before January 2013 receive a higher benefit but pay 
between 15%-20% of the pension cost, while all other employees receive a lower pension benefit and pay 16%-
19% of the annual pension cost). 
Detail of gross projected pension plan costs for FY 2025-27, offset by employee reimbursements, is outlined below.

BUDGET OvERviEW - MAJOR GENERAL FUND TAX BASE PROJECTiONS
Local taxes are projected to account for approximately 
74% of General Fund revenues in FY 2025-26. Five key tax 
sources—Transient Occupancy Taxes (TOT), Sales Taxes, 
Property Taxes, Utility Users Taxes (UUT), and Business 
License Taxes—represent $272.9 million, or 79% of the 
$343.6 million in total local tax revenue expected. The 
remaining tax revenues stem from Parking Facility Taxes, 
Real Property Transfer Taxes, vehicle License Fees, and 
Condominium Taxes.
The revenue projections reflect ongoing challenges in retail, 
tourism, and hospitality—sectors still recovering from the 
pandemic that continue to be impacted by visitor activity 
and consumer spending in Santa Monica. Hybrid work 
trends have reduced the daytime population to about 60% 
of 2019 levels, contributing to higher retail and corporate 
vacancy rates than regional averages, are slowing economic 
recovery. Additionally, international concerns about travel to the U.S. have dampened tourism growth. As always, 
economic uncertainties, including inflation, a potential economic downturn from tariffs and unforeseen state or 
federal legislative changes, could further affect the actual tax revenues received.
Revenue projections use standard methodologies, including trend analysis, fiscal impacts of legislative changes, 
consultant forecasts, and professional judgment, ensuring a prudent approach to forecasting.
PROPERTY TAXES — 19.1% OF GENERAL FUND REVENUES
Total assessed values in the City increased 4% in FY 2024-25, slightly lower than the annual average 6% increase 
over the last five years. While growth is expected to persist, it will likely slow due to a slight softening in the real 
estate market as interest rates remain high. Over the next two years, the City’s revenue growth of about 4% 
annually is expected before returning to a more moderate level of about 3.3% annually.
Revenue from unsecured property taxes (personal property and airplanes) is projected to remain relatively flat 
throughout the next few years, as are delinquent tax receipts and supplemental. Additionally, the City will continue 
to receive residual and pass-through payments from Redevelopment Property Tax Trust Funds (RPTTF) due to the 
dissolution of Redevelopment over ten years ago.

===== PAGE 123 =====

114
SALES TAXES — 16% OF GENERAL FUND REVENUES
Total sales taxes include the standard “Bradley Burns” 1% allocation of taxable sales that all cities receive as well 
as the Transaction and Use Tax (TUT), approved at one-half percent by Santa Monica voters in 2010 (Measure Y) 
and increased to 1% in November 2016 per ballot Measure GSH. Half of the TUT is paid to the Santa Monica−
Malibu Unified School District per a Master Facility Use agreement, following advisory measures approved by 
Santa Monica voters in 2010 and 2016, and another 25% is set aside for affordable housing uses following the 
2016 advisory measure. However, this housing set aside was temporarily suspended during the pandemic-induced 
economic downturn and is anticipated to resume in FY 2025-26.
While visitor activity is recovering, it remains slow, exacerbated by hybrid work trends. These shifts impact visitor 
activity and consumer spending. Sales tax collections have also been impacted by reduced consumer spending in 
retail and other areas, particularly in luxury and electric vehicle sectors. Factors like a 29% rise in car prices since 
the pandemic, higher financing costs, increased interest rates, and insurance premiums have driven these shifts. 
Restaurants, especially in the fine dining sector, are experiencing declines as consumers opt for more budget-
friendly options. This softening economy is anticipated to result in revenues showing essentially no growth in FY 
2025-26 before resuming a modest average annual growth of about 3% beginning in FY 2026-27.
TRANSIENT OCCUPANCY TAXES — 15.6% OF GENERAL FUND REVENUES
Forecasts for Transient Occupancy Taxes (TOT) have been developed in coordination with Santa Monica Travel and 
Tourism (SMTT). Although visitor activity has been steadily recovering, the pace remains slow. The Los Angeles 
wildfires and geopolitics have resulted in a slower rebound in tourism, TOT revenue recovery remains sluggish 
compared to pre-pandemic projections. Despite Measure CS, enacted in March 2023, which raised the TOT by 1% 
for hotels and 3% for homeshares, the revenue rebound has been slower than anticipated. Recent projections 
indicate the city may not reach peak 2019 tourism levels until late 2026 or early 2027, as tourism gradually 
rebounds. As of April 2025, local hotel average daily rate is lower with occupancy hovering around 70% citywide. 
Based on preliminary data from the Department of Commerce, U.S. Customs and Border Protection and outside 
organizations, international visits to the United States fell approximately 14% in March 2025 compared to the 
same period last year.  SMTT indicates that for every 1% drop in international visitor spending approximately $1.8 
billion is lost in export revenue annually. Therefore, if the 14% decline were to hold through 2025, the U.S. stands 
to lose $21 billion in travel-related exports.  These factors could further impact TOT revenue projections.
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
BUDGET OvERviEW - MAJOR GENERAL FUND TAX BASE PROJECTiONS


===== PAGE 124 =====

115
UTILITY USERS TAXES — 8% OF GENERAL FUND REVENUES
Utility Users Taxes (UUT) are levied on the consumption of utilities such as electric, gas, and cable television. UUT 
growth is expected to be modest in the coming years at a rate of approximately 2% annually. This anticipated 
increase is due to water and wastewater rate increases which are expected to be partially offset by continuing 
decreases in taxes from hard wire, wireless telecommunications services and cable subscribers, reflecting a 
shift of consumer use to more internet based, non-taxable services. There is significant litigation underway that 
may impact the imposition of UUT on streaming video services. Staff will continue to monitor the status of the 
litigation.  
BUSINESS LICENSE TAXES — 7.8% OF GENERAL FUND REVENUES
Back-to-back years of historic Business License Tax (BLT) compliance efforts and significant increase in tax 
payments from our largest businesses resulted in FY 2024-25 business license tax revenue exceeding pre-
pandemic levels. Though the pandemic exacerbated the downward shift in brick and mortal businesses, gross 
receipts reported, and business license taxes collected have exceeded all previous years.
Measure F is expected to result in BLT revenue increase of approximately $3 million annually once fully 
implemented. Business license tax changes and penalty reductions will take effect in FY 2025-26, while the tax 
rate increases will be phased in over three years with the final increase being implemented in FY 2027-28. After 
Measure F is fully implemented, it is anticipated that business license tax growth will soften and is expected to 
level off to 2% annual increases. 
PARKING FACILITY TAXES — 3.9% OF GENERAL FUND REVENUES
Parking revenues, another significant revenue source affected by visitors, are experiencing an average annual 
growth of 1.75%. The pandemic accelerated many trends, including online shopping and remote working, that 
will likely impact visitor behavior and decrease parking demand in future years. Staff anticipates that demand for 
parking will continue to flatten or decline as consumers adopt alternative transportation methods, as well as the 
increased use of free parking in the City’s downtown structures (currently for the first 90 minutes).  Parking Facility 
Taxes (PFT), which are assessed on private and public parking fees in the city, are projected to increase over the 
next two years.  This is primarily due to Tax Measure K, enacted in January 2025, which increases the PFT by 8% 
for privately owned facilities, bringing the total tax to 18% and is anticipated to generate $6.7 million in annual 
revenues.
REAL PROPERTY (DOCUMENTARY) TRANSFER TAXES — 2.7% OF GENERAL 
FUND REVENUES
Real Property (Documentary) Transfer Tax revenues are very volatile, economy driven and can be skewed by very 
large-value transfers. The real estate market continued to exhibit strength during the pandemic as the number 
of annual property transfers increased sharply after dipping slightly at the onset of the pandemic. The volume of 
transfers dropped 44% and has remained relatively flat over the last couple of years, and tax receipts have shown 
a similar pattern over the same time period. The impact of Measure GS, approved by the voters in November 2022, 
added a third tier of the tax at $56 per $1,000 sale value for transfers $8 million. Revenues from the increase are 
deposited into the Measure GS Fund as the use of the funds are restricted.

FUND BALANCES

===== PAGE 126 =====

116
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
FUND BALANCES - CLASSiFiCATiON OF FUNDS
The financial operations of the City are organized into funds for which budgets are prepared. 
These funds are grouped into three major categories: General, Capital Projects and Special 
Revenue, and Proprietary.
This section includes a description of the funds and their fund balance projections for the two 
budget years.
THE GENERAL FUND
The General Fund (01) is used to account for all financial resources necessary to carry out basic governmental 
activities of the City that are not accounted for in another fund. The General Fund supports essential City services 
such as police and fire protection, street maintenance, libraries, parks, and open space management. Most of the 
General Fund is financed from tax revenues.
 
CAPITAL PROJECTS AND SPECIAL REVENUE FUNDS
Capital Projects and Special Revenue Funds are used to account for specific revenues that are generally legally 
restricted to expenditures for specified purposes.
• Asset Seizure Fund (21) — To account for the receipt and expenditure of federal and state asset 
forfeiture funds from equitable sharing programs. The federal guideline disallows a grantee to supplant its 
budget with asset seizure funds. 
• Beach Recreation Fund (11) — To account for beach parking, concession, and beach house revenues 
and expenditures related to beach maintenance and recreation activities.
• Citizens Option for Public Safety (COPS) Fund (22) — To account for the receipt and expenditure of 
the Citizens Option for Public Safety program established by AB3229 of 1996. in addition to disallowing a 
grantee to use the funds to supplant its budget, the State guideline requires a public hearing for the use 
of funds. Appropriations are recommended to the Council by a separate staff report after the hearing is 
conducted.
• Clean Beaches and Ocean Parcel Tax Fund (16) — To account for activity related to implementation of 
Watershed Management Plan and the passage of Measure v in November 2006.
• Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) Fund (19) — To account for Federal entitlements under 
the Housing and Community Development Act of 1974, as amended. The City Council annually allocates 
CDBG funds to various programs.
• Gas Tax Fund (26) — To account for State and County gasoline tax allocations and any Federal funds 
provided to the City for street-related purposes.


===== PAGE 127 =====

117
• Housing Authority Fund (12) — To account for the receipt and expenditure of federal funds related to 
housing programs.
• Local Return Fund (27) —  To account for revenues and expenditures from the Proposition A, Proposition 
C and Measure R Local Return programs, three one-half cent sales tax measures approved by Los Angeles 
County voters to finance a countywide transit development program.
• Low- and Moderate-Income Housing Asset Fund (41) — To account for the revenues and expenditures 
formerly in the Low- and Moderate-income Housing Fund established under Community Redevelopment 
law.
• Measure GS Fund (29) — To account for receipt and expenditure of a third-tier documentary transfer tax 
of an additional 5% for property sales over $8 million with the revenue restricted to fund homelessness 
prevention, affordable housing projects, and schools.
• Miscellaneous Grants Fund (20) — To account for the receipt and expenditure of miscellaneous Federal, 
State and County awarded grants and special allocations provided to the City.
• Parks and Recreation Fund (28) — To account for funds collected under the City's Unit Dwelling Tax. 
These funds are to be used for the acquisition, improvement, and expansion of public parks, playgrounds, 
and recreational facilities.
• Rent Control Fund (25) — To account for revenues and expenditures of funds that are restricted or 
committed for Rent Control activities. Though included in the Annual Comprehensive Financial Report, the 
fund is not discussed in this document, as the budget is prepared separately for the Rent Control Board’s 
adoption.
 
• South Coast Air Quality Management District (SCAQMD) Fund (18) — To account for the receipt of 
Air Quality Management District revenues and eligible expenditures.
• Special Revenue Source Fund (10) — To account for receipt and expenditure of monies restricted, 
committed or assigned for specific use.
• Tenant Ownership Rights Charter Amendment (TORCA) Fund (14) — To account for filing fee and 
conversion tax revenues and expenditures related to various housing programs authorized by Chapter XX 
of the City Charter.
PROPRIETARY (ENTERPRISE AND INTERNAL SERVICE) FUNDS
Proprietary (Enterprise and internal Service) Funds are used to account for operations that are financed and 
operated in a manner similar to private business enterprises where (a) the intent is that the costs (expenses, 
including depreciation) of providing goods or services to the general public or City departments on a continuing 
basis be financed or recovered primarily through user charges, or (b) the City has decided that periodic 
determination of revenues earned, expenses incurred and/or net income is appropriate for capital maintenance, 
public policy, management control, accountability, or other purposes.
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
FUND BALANCES - CLASSiFiCATiON OF FUNDS


===== PAGE 128 =====

118
• Airport Fund (57) — To account for revenues and expenses connected with management of the Santa 
Monica Municipal Airport.
• Big Blue Bus (BBB) Fund (60) — To account for revenues and expenses related to operation of the City's 
municipal bus lines.
• Cemetery Fund (59) — To account for revenues and expenses associated with operation of Woodlawn 
Cemetery and Mausoleum.
• Information Technology Replacement and Services Fund (71) — To account for user charges from 
other funds and expenses related to replacement of computer and telecommunication equipment.
• Parking Authority Fund (61) — The Parking Authority has been a financing authority for the City's 
parking structures.  
• Pier Fund (53) — To account for revenues and expenses connected with management and development 
of the Santa Monica Pier.
• Resource Recovery and Recycling (RRR) Fund (54) — To account for revenues and expenses of 
operating the City's refuse collection, street sweeping and cleaning, and recycling programs.
• Self-insurance, Bus Fund (73) — To account for contributions from the Big Blue Bus Fund and expenses 
related to the administration and payment of bus-related liability claims.
• Self-insurance, General Liability and Auto Fund (72) — To account for user charges from other funds 
and expenses related to the administration and payment of general liability and auto claims.
• Self-insurance, Risk Management Administration Fund (74) — To account for user charges from 
other funds and expenses related to the administration of the Risk Management Division.
• Self-insurance, Workers’ Compensation Fund (75) — To account for contributions from City 
Departments for administration and payment of workers' compensation claims.
• Stormwater Management Fund (52) — To account for revenues and expenses associated with 
stormwater management.
• Vehicle Management Fund (70) — To account for user charges from other funds and expenses related 
to replacement, maintenance and fueling of City-owned vehicles, including specialized mechanical 
equipment.
• Wastewater Fund (51) — To account for revenues and expenses associated with maintaining the 
sanitary sewer and storm drain systems within the City.
• Water Fund (50) — To account for revenues and expenses of providing water service. 
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
FUND BALANCES - CLASSiFiCATiON OF FUNDS


===== PAGE 129 =====

119
Three other fund types that are included in the City’s Annual Comprehensive Financial Report are not budgeted for 
various reasons:
FIDUCIARY FUNDS
Fiduciary Funds are used to account for resources held for the benefit of parties outside the City. The fund 
resources are not available to support City programs and therefore they are not budgeted.
• Custodial Fund (80) — To account for assets held by the City as a trustee or as an agent for individuals 
or other governmental units.
• Private Purpose Trust Fund (43) — To account for the distribution of assets of the dissolved 
Redevelopment Agency of the City of Santa Monica.
• Street Light Fund (83) — To account for collected payments of street assessments.
PERMANENT FUNDS
Permanent Funds, which consist of Cemetery Perpetual Care (81) Fund and Mausoleum Perpetual Care (82) Fund, 
are used to report resources that reflect only earnings, not principal, to be used for City programs. Earnings from 
these two funds are transferred to the Cemetery Fund to support the cemetery operations. While the two perpetual 
care funds are not budgeted, the Cemetery Fund budgets the transfer amounts.
DEBT SERVICE FUND
Debt Service Fund is used to account for the accumulation of resources for, and the payment of, general long-term 
debt principal, interest and related costs for public facilities.

The following section shows appropriable fund balance projections for each year of the biennial

yearly basis. in the cases where budgeted expenditures exceed revenues, this indicates one-time 
capital projects where expenditures are funded from prior years’ revenue held in reserve for this 
purpose, except in the case of the Housing Authority, which requires a transfer of funds from the 
Special Revenue Source Fund.

133
FY 2025-27 PROPOSED BiENNiAL BUDGET
REvENUES - OvERviEW
OVERVIEW
Departmental staff works in concert with Finance Department staff to develop revenue projections.  The projections 
reflect a prudent and consistent, approach using established budget practices that reflect the economic impacts 
described in the “Overall Economic Conditions” section of the budget.  Staff considers qualitative and quantitative 
methods of forecasting and uses techniques such as trend analysis, economic indicators, and professional 
judgment, to arrive at the revenue projections.  Revenue forecasting is always one of the most challenging 
aspects in the budget process, with many unknown variables over which the City has little or no control including 
fluctuations in the local, regional, statewide, and national economies; patterns of consumer habits and demands; 
geo-political events, and the fiscal impacts of legislative changes.  
TOTAL CITY REVENUES
Total City revised budgeted revenues for FY 2024-25, after 
adjustments for reimbursements and transfers between funds, 
are $775.5 million reflecting a $2.1 million, or 0.3%, increase 
over FY 2023-24.
FY 2025-26 total City revenues, after adjustments for 
reimbursements and transfers between funds, are projected 
to be $810.2 million, $34.8 million or 4.5% more than the FY 
2024-25 budget. The increase reflects greater General Fund 
revenues ($14.5 million) primarily from various taxes including 
Property, Parking Facility and Business License taxes ($11.1 
million) and revenue from the City’s Digital Wayfinding Kiosk 
program($2.9 million).  Other increases include revenues from the Miscellaneous Grants Fund ($8.4 million) 
reflecting the timing of estimated reimbursement revenues from grant expenses, the Water Fund ($7.7 million) and 
Wastewater Fund ($3.3 million) from projected water and wastewater rate adjustments anticipated to take effect 
in July 2025, the Resource Recovery and Recycling Fund ($5.5 million) from approved solid waste rate adjustments,  
and the Housing Authority Fund ($1.8 million) reflecting increased use and associated funding allocation of 
Federal Housing Choice voucher (Section 8) Program vouchers.  The increases are primarily offset by decreases 
in the Big Blue Bus Fund ($1.5 million) reflecting a decrease in transportation funding allocations from LA Metro 
due to a decline in sales tax revenue, the Beach Fund ($1.1 million) primarily due to reduced parking capacity 
resulting from the planned use of 50% of the 1550 Beach lot for construction staging in preparation for the Pier 
Bridge Replacement project – slated to begin in summer 2026, and the Airport Fund ($1.9 million) due to newly 
negotiated tenant lease credits and anticipated vacancies in commercial buildings and a former Santa Monica 
College building. All other funds are expected to show a net revenue decrease of $1.9 million.  
FY 2026-27 total City revenues, after adjustments for reimbursements and transfers between funds, are projected 
to be $854.9 million, $44.7 million or 5.5% more than in FY 2025-26.  The increase is primarily driven by greater 
General Fund revenues from continued implementation of the Business License Tax rate changes, moderate growth 
in Property Taxes, as well as slight growth in Sales Tax and Transient Occupancy tax from some return of visitors

and tourism due to anticipated major events in the region ($18.0 million); increases in the Airport Fund ($5.0 
million) as office tenancy is expected to return, increases in the Big Blue Bus Fund ($14.9 million) from increased 
capital project funding, increases in the Resource Recovery and Recycling Fund ($3.4 million) from approved 
solid waste rate adjustments, and increases in the Water Fund ($7.9 million) and Wastewater Fund ($2.4 million) 
reflecting projected water and wastewater rate adjustments.  The increases are primarily offset by a decrease in 
expected grant funding in the Miscellaneous Grants Fund ($5.4 million).  All other funds are expected to show net 
revenue decrease of $1.5 million.
FUND DETAILS
Revenue projections for the next two fiscal years for all City funds are detailed in the section that follows.
GENERAL FUND
 The FY 2024-25 revised revenue budget is $455.9 million, 
$7.0 million (1.6%) more than in FY 2023-24.  The revenue 
projections reflect ongoing challenges in retail, tourism, and 
hospitality—sectors still recovering from the pandemic that 
continue to be impacted by visitor activity and consumer 
spending in Santa Monica. Hybrid work trends have reduced the 
daytime population to about 60% of 2019 levels, contributing to 
higher retail and corporate vacancy rates than regional averages, 
these factors are slowing economic recovery. Additionally, 
international concerns about travel to the U.S. have dampened 
tourism growth. As always, economic uncertainties, including 
inflation, a potential economic downturn from tariffs and 
unforeseen state or federal legislative changes, could further affect the actual tax revenues received. These factors 
are impacting revenues in the General Fund.  
FY 2025-26 total General Fund revenues are projected to be $470.4 million, which is $14.5 million (3.2%) greater 
than the FY 2024-25 revised budget. The increase is primarily from gains in Parking Facility Taxes ($4.1 million) 
due to voter approved Measure K, enacted in January 2025, which increases the PFT by 8% for privately owned 
facilities, Property Taxes ($3.5 million) reflecting a slight softening in the real estate market as interest rates remain 
high, Transient Occupancy Taxes ($3.2 million) reflecting slight increases in tourism levels, increased advertising 
revenue from full implementation of the City’s Digital Wayfinding Kiosk program ($2.9 million), and a net increase 
of $0.8 million from various other sources. 
FY 2026-27 revenues are forecast to be $461.5 million, $17.2 million (3.9%) more than in FY 2025-26. The 
increases are from Transient Occupancy Taxes ($4.6 million) reflecting moderate growth projected for Transient 
Occupancy tax revenue from some return of visitors and tourism due to anticipated major events in the region, 
Property Taxes ($3.7 million) reflecting a more typical  growth of about 3.3% annually, Sales Taxes ($2.0 million) 
reflecting a modest average annual growth of about 3%, Business License taxes ($1.2 million) reflecting tax rate 
changes (from approved Measure F) phased in over three years, other taxes ($1.1 million), and a net increase of 
$4.6 million from  various other revenues.

General Fund revenues are grouped into the following major revenue categories.  An in-depth review of each of 
these major revenue categories follows:
 • Property Taxes       • Licenses & Permits
 • Sales Taxes       • Fines/Forfeitures
 • Other Taxes       • Charges for Services
  ◊ Utility Users Taxes     • investment income (interest)
  ◊ Transient Occupancy Taxes    • Rent
  ◊ Business Licenses Taxes     • intergovernmental
  ◊ Other Local Taxes     • Other
PROPERTY TAXES
Property Tax revenues result from a 1% levy on the assessed 
value of all real property in the City.  Proposition 13, passed 
by California voters in 1979, specifies that assessed value of 
properties will change at the rate of the Consumer Price index, 
not to exceed 2% per year, unless the property is improved or 
sold, thus establishing a new market value.
The 1% Property Tax levy is collected by the Los Angeles County 
Tax Collector and is distributed to various public agencies located 
in the County, including cities, school districts, and special 
districts.  Santa Monica’s share of the 1% levy is approximately 
14% to 17%, depending on the area of the City where the 
property is located.
The primary component of Property Taxes is taxes secured by real property.  The City also receives unsecured 
property taxes, a reimbursement of homeowners’ exemptions, pass through and residual tax payments related 
to the former Redevelopment Agency (RDA), and an additional levy over the 1% rate used to pay debt service on 
voter approved debt.  Beginning in FY 2004-05, this category also includes property taxes received in exchange for 
lost vehicle License Fee (vLF) revenue resulting from the decrease in the vLF rate per the FY 2004-05 State budget 
(vLF Swap).
The FY 2024-25 revised budget for Property Tax revenues is $2.2 million more than in FY 2023-24, primarily due to 
increases in assessed valuation growth and residual Property Tax payments related to the former RDA. 
Property Taxes are projected to increase in FY 2025-26 and FY 2026-27 by $3.5 million and $3.7 million, 
respectively, primarily from assessed valuation increases that are expected to continue and greater residual 
Property Tax payments related to the former RDA.

SALES TAXES
The total sales tax rate in Santa Monica is 10.25%.  The rate is 
allocated as follows:
State  6.00% 
Local   1.00% City General Fund
  0.25% To Counties for Transportation Uses
Measure A 0.5%    To Counties to Address Homelessness -   
   effective 4/1/2025
  
Prop A  0.5% 
Prop C  0.5% 
Measure R  0.5% 
Measure M 0.5% 
  
Measures Y 1.00% Measure Y approved by Santa Monica voters in November 2010 added 0.5% and Measure  
and GSH  GSH approved by Santa Monica voters in November 2016 added an additional 0.5% to the  
   rate.   
One half of the Measure Y and GSH Transaction and Use Tax revenues are paid to the Santa Monica-Malibu Unified 
School District for use of certain school district facilities.  Additionally, 25% of the Measure GSH revenues are set 
aside to be used for affordable housing. 
Sales tax revenues are gradually rebounding from pandemic lows and were tracking to exceed pre-COviD levels by 
FY 2024-25. However, due to shifts in consumer spending, slowing tourism and a softening economy FY 2023-24 
experienced a 7.3% decline as compared to FY 2022-23 actuals. The FY 2024-25 revised budget is projected to be 
a modest $2.5 million (nearly 3.4%) over FY 2023-24.
The uncertainties in the economy are anticipated to continue into FY 2025-26, and revenues are projected to be 
flat with FY 2024-25, while increasing  by $2.0 million (2.7%) in FY 2026-27.

UTILITY USERS TAXES
The City’s 10% Utility Users Tax (UUT) is applied to electricity, 
natural gas, telephone (both hardwire and wireless), cable, and 
water/wastewater services.
FY 2024-25 revised budget revenue projections are  
approximately 3.7% more than in FY 2023-24, primarily due to 
higher energy costs and  increases in electricity and gas rates. 
Utility Users Taxes (UUT) are projected to increase by $0.3 million 
(0.9%) in FY 2025-26 and another $0.5 million in FY 2026-27 
(1.4%) primarily due to anticipated Council-approved water and 
wastewater rate increases, partially offset by the continuing decrease in revenues from telecommunication services 
as consumers shift to more internet-based services which are not subject to the tax.
TRANSIENT OCCUPANCY TAX
The City levies a tax on transient room rentals of 15% for hotel/
motel stays and 17% for homeshare stays. 
Tourism, one of the primary drivers of the City’s economy, 
experienced a fast recovery immediately after the devastating 
impacts of the COviD-19 pandemic. However, lingering effects 
of the pandemic as well as the LA wildfires and geopolitics have 
resulted in a slowed rebound in tourism, projected to reach 
2019 levels only by late 2026 or early 2027. The FY 2024-25 
revised budget is 6.3% more than FY 2023-24 actuals.  The 
increase reflects higher average room rates and occupancy levels, 
although occupancy still lags pre-COviD levels, as international 
and business travel have been slower to recover than domestic leisure travel. 
FY 2025-26 and FY 2026-27 increases of approximately $3.2 million and $4.6 million, respectively, reflect modest 
increases in the average daily room rate and occupancy. in addition, major sports events coming to the region, 
including the FiFA World Cup in 2026 and the Superbowl in 2027, are expected to attract more visitors to Santa 
Monica.

BUSINESS LICENSE TAXES
Businesses operating in the City of Santa Monica are required to 
obtain a business license and pay a tax based on a percentage 
of gross receipts (revenues), the cost of operations, or as a flat 
amount, depending on business type annually.  in most cases, 
the amount of the tax is based on prior calendar year gross  
receipts that are attributable to the business activity conducted 
in Santa Monica. 
in November 2024, voters approved Measure F , which 
modernizes the business license tax provisions of the Santa 
Monica Municipal Code for the first time since 1990. Measure 
F impacts businesses by expanding exemptions, reduced taxes for small property owners, new businesses and 
retailers and restaurants generating less than $5 million in gross revenue annually, and aligned tax rates for  auto 
industry and corporate administrative headquarters  with neighboring jurisdictions. The modernized rates ranges 
from  $1.15 to $5.00 for each $1,000 in gross receipts or cost of operations.
Revenues for FY 2025-26 are expected to decline by approximately 1% ($0.4 million) compared to FY 2024-25 due 
to substantial one-time business license tax payments received in FY 2024-25 and upcoming penalty reductions 
set to take effect next fiscal year. Tax rate increases will be phased in over three years with the final increase being 
implemented in FY 2027-28. FY 2026-27 revenues, projected to increase by 3.3% ($1.3 million) in FY 2026-27, 
reflecting continued implementation of rate changes associated with Measure F .

OTHER TAXES
Other Taxes include: 
Real Property (Documentary) Transfer Tax 
The Documentary Transfer Tax is levied on the sale of real 
property in the City at a rate of $3.00 per $1,000 of transfer 
value for transfers valued under $5 Million.  Per Measure SM, 
approved by Santa Monica voters in November 2020, transfers 
valued $5 million and over are assessed a tax of $6.00 per 
$1,000 of transfer value.  Measure GS, approved by voters in 
November 2022 added a third tier to the tax of $56.00 per 
$1,000 for transfers valued at $8 million and over, effective 
March 1, 2023.  The incremental revenues from the third tier are 
restricted as to use and deposited into the Measure GS Fund.
Vehicle License Fees (VLF) 
The State Budget Act of 2011 ended the allocation of vLF to cities except for a small portion related to certain 
compliance procedures.
Parking Facility Tax 
The Parking Facility Tax of 10% is assessed on private and public parking fees collected in the City. Measure K, 
approved by voters in November 2024, raised the tax rate in private parking lots and structures to 18% effective 
January 1, 2025.
Condominium Tax 
The Condominium Tax of $1,000 per unit is assessed on all new condominium construction and condominium 
conversions in the City.
The FY 2025-26 projected increase of $4.5 million primarily reflects the full year of Parking Facility Taxes.
FY 2026-27 revenues are projected to increase an additional $0.6 million reflecting increased Documentary 
Transfer Taxes as the economy improves while Parking Facility Taxes are expected to level off after full 
implementation of the Measure K tax increase .

LICENSES AND PERMITS
The primary component of the Licenses and Permits revenue 
category is parking revenues, which are a significant revenue 
source affected by visitors.  The pandemic accelerated many 
trends, including online shopping and remote working, that will 
likely impact visitor behavior and decrease parking demand 
in future years. Staff anticipate that demand for parking will 
continue to flatten or decline as consumers adopt alternative 
transportation methods, as well as the increased use of free 
parking in the City’s downtown structures (currently for the first 
90 minutes).  The FY 2024-25 revised revenue budget is about 
2% or $0.6 million above FY 2023-24 actuals.
Revenues are expected to increase slightly in FY 2025-26, resulting in a $0.3 million (0.7%) increase, followed by 
an additional $0.6 million (1.7%) increase in FY 2026-27. The revenue projections do not include the impact of any 
potential parking rate changes that would be presented to the City Council and submitted to the California Coastal 
Commission for approval in late 2025.
FINES AND FORFEITURES
Fines and forfeitures are comprised mainly of parking citation 
fines but also include administrative fines and penalties from 
Code Enforcement and Police Department activities, California 
vehicle code fines, library fines, and other administrative fines. 
in total, the FY 2024-25 revised revenue budget of $14.4 million 
is up approximately 19% from FY 2023-24 reflecting a one-time 
adjustment for prior year revenue.  
Revenues are projected to return to approximately $13.3 million 
in FY 2025-26 and remain relatively flat in FY 2026-27.

CHARGES FOR SERVICES
The FY 2024-25 revised revenue budget of $54.2 million is $11.3 
million below FY 2023-24, primarily due to a one-time insurance 
recovery payment received in FY 2023-24 for settlements related 
to sexual abuse claims.  The decreases were partially mitigated 
by new revenues received from the City’s Digital Wayfinding 
Kiosk program, and greater than anticipated revenues related to 
mutual aid deployments by the Fire Department to assist with 
wildfires as part of multiple mutual aid strike team deployments 
and mobile stroke unit special deployments (these are typically 
unbudgeted reimbursements that fluctuate year over year).
Revenues are projected to increase by $3.7 million in FY 2025-
26, primarily due to the advertising revenue from a full year of the Digital Wayfinding Kiosk program. This increase 
is partially offset by a reduction to mutual aid deployment revenues.  
in FY 2026-27 revenues are projected to increase by $1.5 million to $59.4 million primarily due to an increase in 
reimbursements from other funds for general government administrative support, as part of the cost allocation 
plan, based on projected total compensation costs.  
INVESTMENT INCOME
Total investment income represents earnings from the City’s 
pooled investment portfolio (which primarily consists of reserves) 
and bond/loan proceeds.  Revenues are budgeted on a cash basis 
and do not consider unrealized gains or losses.  Additionally, 
interest on bond proceeds is not typically included in the budget 
since it is restricted to the projects for which the bonds were 
issued.  The chart and the discussion exclude unrealized gains 
and losses, other year-end accounting adjustments, and interest 
from bond/loan proceeds.
FY 2024-25 revised budget revenues are $1.4 million less than 
in FY 2023-24. Due to the fluctuations of City investments and 
interest rates, the amount budgeted for interest income is typically conservative. However, with the sharp rise in 
interest rates over the past couple of years, interest income has surpassed the budgeted amount for FY 2024-25. 
 
FY 2025-26 and FY 2026-27 revenues are projected to be flat as compared to FY 2024-25 projected actuals, 
as interest rates have leveled. The overall portfolio yield is expected to increase slightly as many investments 
purchased before the rise in rates will mature and be reinvested at much higher rates of return, however economic 
indicators have indicated there will likely be a lowering of rates over the short-term.

OTHER REVENUES
The FY 2024-25 revised revenue budget of $3.8 million is $1.1 
million more than in FY 2023-24 primarily due to the discount the 
City receives for pre-paying the full employer share of its CalPERS 
retirement contribution at the beginning of each fiscal year, which 
has resumed after being suspended in FY 2023-24.  
Other revenues are projected to remain relatively flat in FY 2025-
26 and increase by approximately $2.3 million in FY 2026-27. The 
increase is primarily related to anticipated developer payments from 
the Miramar Hotel during the period it is closed for renovation to 
partially offset lost Transient Occupancy Taxes.

REvENUES - REvENUE ANALYSiS, OTHER FUNDS
AIRPORT FUND 
This enterprise fund accounts for revenues and expenses 
connected with management of the Santa Monica Municipal 
Airport and its property.  
FY 2024-25 revised budgeted revenues of $14.8 million reflect 
a decrease of $4.3 million from FY 2023-24, primarily due to 
unanticipated property vacancies, delays in building renovations, 
tenant improvement credits on newly negotiated leases, and the 
repurposing of a former rental property.
Revenues are projected to decline in FY 2025-26 due to 
the continuation of tenant lease credits and vacancies in 
commercial buildings and a former Santa Monica College building.  Revenues in FY 2026-27 are projected to 
increase by $5.0 million once renovations are complete and lease revenues are reinstated.
BEACH RECREATION FUND
 
This special revenue fund accounts for revenues and expenses 
related to beach parking, recreation activities, concession 
revenues, maintenance, and Beach House operations. Beach 
parking revenues make up approximately 80% of total income 
and are highly dependent on weather conditions and other 
external factors.
For FY 2025-26, proposed budgeted revenues are $15 million, 
reflecting a $1.1 million decrease from FY 2024-25. This decline 
is primarily attributed to the Pier Bridge closure in the summer 
of 2026, which will result in 50% closure of the 1550 Beach 
parking lot for construction staging related to the project.
Revenues for FY 2026-27 are projected to decline further, by an additional $1 million, to $14.8 million, also due 
to the Pier Bridge closure. Staff will revisit rate increases upon completion of the ongoing citywide parking rate 
study. Any adjustments to parking rates will require approval from the California Coastal Commission (CCC), a 
prerequisite for implementation.

BIG BLUE BUS FUND 
This enterprise fund accounts for operating and capital 
revenues and expenses related to operation of the City's 
municipal bus lines. 
FY 2024-25 revised budgeted revenues of $122 million are 
$30 million higher than FY 2023-24 primarily due to projected 
reimbursement revenue from funding agencies associated with 
capital project expenses.
FY 2025-26 proposed budgeted revenues of $120.6 million are 
$1.5 million lower than FY 2024-25, primarily due to a decrease 
in the funding allocation by LA Metro as a result of lower than 
anticipated County and State-wide sales tax revenues.
FY 2026-27 revenues are projected to increase by $14.9 million to $135.5 million due to the projected increases in 
funding allocations by LA Metro and reimbursements associated with capital project expenses, which includes the 
procurement of battery-electric buses (BEB) and associated electrical infrastructure.  
CEMETERY FUND
 
This enterprise fund accounts for revenues and expenses 
associated with the operation of the City of Santa Monica 
Woodlawn Cemetery, Mausoleum, and Mortuary.
FY 2024-25 revised budgeted revenues of $2.4 million are $0.2 
million lower than FY 2023-24, reflecting a decrease in cemetery 
and mausoleum-related sales. 
FY 2025-26 revenues are expected to increase by approximately 
$0.3 million to $2.7 million pending fee increases to recover the 
increased cost of landscaping services and other miscellaneous 
costs. 
FY 2026-27 revenues are anticipated to remain relatively flat at $2.8 million.

This fund accounts for State-funded revenues and associated 
expenditures for the Citizens' Option for Public Safety (COPS) 
program established per AB 3229 of 1996. 
FY 2025-26 proposed budgeted revenues of $245,000 are 
relatively level with FY 2024-25.  
FY 2026-27 revenues are projected to continue to remain flat 
with FY 2025-26. 
CLEAN BEACHES AND OCEAN PARCEL TAX FUND 
This fund records activity related to implementation of the 
Watershed Management Plan and the passage of Measure v, 
Clean Beaches and Ocean Parcel Tax, in November 2006. The 
assessment rate is subject to annual CPi increases.
FY 2024-25 revised budgeted revenues of $4.7 million are $0.5

from lower average fund balances contributing to the City’s 
investment pooled portfolio. 
FY 2025-26 revenues are projected to increase by $0.4 million 
to $5.0 million reflecting modest increases in parcel tax revenues subject to CPi adjustments.  
FY 2026-27 revenues are expected to remain relatively level.

COMMUNITY BROADBAND FUND 
This enterprise fund previously recorded revenues and 
expenses related to the management of Santa Monica CityNet, 
the City’s 100 Gigabit advanced broadband initiative. Effective 
FY 2023-24, the Community Broadband Fund was consolidated 
into the General Fund, realigning the structure of CityNet 
operations as a way to control costs.
COMMUNITY DEVELOPMENT BLOCK GRANT (CDBG) FUND 
 
The Community Development Block Grant (CBDG) Fund 
accounts for Federal entitlements under the Housing and 
Community Development Act of 1974 (as amended).  The City 
Council annually allocates CDBG funds to various programs.
The revenues for the biennial period are projected to remain 
flat at the current funding level of $1.2 million.
